Documents

  • Resolution #25-41: Establishing an Affiliated Tribes of Northwest Indians (ATNI) Task Force on Data Centers (PDF, on atnitribes.org)
  • Resolution #2026-034: Endorsement of the Tiered Sovereign Data Framework as an ATNI-Recognized Standard for Indigenous Data Sovereignty (PDF)
  • Resolution #2026-039: The ATNI Energy Committee Supports the Formation of a Task Force on Artificial Intelligence (PDF)
  • Resolution #2024-35: Calling for Tribal Leadership and Oversight in Electric Grid Governance and Electric Transmission Planning (PDF)
  • climate-regions.json, the county climate projections, derived from NASA’s NEX-GDDP-CMIP6 dataset under its own terms (see License)
  • projects.json, every named proposal as its public filings state it, and projects.svg, the county map
  • comparison-model.json, the comparison model several of these plates run on
  • policy-watch.json, the saved searches and follow lists on Policy Watch, and the follow lists Policy Sentinel can import: follow-ai.json, follow-data-centers.json, and follow-energy-feasibility.json
  • GeoBase and the Tiered Sovereign Data Framework (GitHub)

Sources by Plate

12 plates; 704 sources from 77 documents: 117 verified and 587 partly verified.

The Council region

The Council puts data centers and chip fabs at 2,475 aMW in 2030, in a region using 22,000 today

17 sources from 8 documents: 12 verified and 5 partly verified. Open the plate. Data: dc-pnw-load.json, with its claims in demand.json.

  1. 9th Plan data center forecast final 03.16.2025.xlsx (Tech-Data Center Loads folder, Ninth Plan Demand Forecast Public Workbooks)

    Northwest Power and Conservation Council, published 03/16/2025. Agency figure.

    Verified against the source.

    ClaimMeasureFigureValue yearQuoteArithmetic
    Boundary: Council tech load forecast for the Pacific Northwest (Oregon, Idaho, Washington, Western Montana) in average MW: data centers plus chip fabs together ('All tech'). The Appendix says the input is incremental data center demand starting from 2024, but the sheet's 2024 row is not zero (low 168.9, mid 259.2, high 351.9), so whether the printed series is a level or an increment over an earlier base is not stated; read the values as printed.
    dem-073Pacific Northwest data centers and chip fabs, NW Council Ninth Plan tech load forecast (workbook), low, mid, high: Tech load input, 2030 (low, mid, high)2,475.1 (1,512.8 to 4,988.7) aMW2030Sheet '20y regional forecasts', row 2030: Low 1512.8, Mid 2475.1, High 4988.7 (workbook cells C10, D10, E10; rounded to one decimal)
    dem-074Pacific Northwest data centers and chip fabs, NW Council Ninth Plan tech load forecast (workbook), low, mid, high: Tech load input, 2046 (low, mid, high)2,958.4 (1,589.3 to 6,923.5) aMW2046Sheet '20y regional forecasts', row 2046: Low 1589.3, Mid 2958.4, High 6923.5 (workbook cells C26, D26, E26; rounded to one decimal)Mid 2046 less mid 2025 (530.4) = 2,428 aMW added over 2025 to 2046. Appendix F Table F-13 gives data centers 20.5% of Mixed Bag growth, and 20.5% x 11,937 = 2,447 aMW (our arithmetic), within about 1% of 2,428.
    dem-076Pacific Northwest data centers and chip fabs, NW Council Ninth Plan tech load forecast (workbook), mid: Tech load input, 2029 (mid)2,197.8 aMW2029Sheet '20y regional forecasts', row 2029: Low 1332.7, Mid 2197.8, High 4660.0 (workbook cells C9, D9, E9; rounded to one decimal)
    Boundary: Council tech load forecast, 'DC only' block (data centers without chip fabs) for the Pacific Northwest, summed over the Council's utility zones. The workbook gives the data-center-only split through 2030 only; the 20-year sheet to 2046 is all tech.
    dem-075Pacific Northwest data centers and chip fabs, NW Council Ninth Plan tech load forecast (workbook), data centers only, low, mid, high: Data center only load input, 2030 (low, mid, high)2,020.4 (1,301.9 to 4,429.3) aMW2030Sheet 'Forecast pathways', 'BASE (DC only)' block, row 2030, Total column: 2020.4; 'High (DC only)' Total 4429.3; 'Low (DC only)' Total 1301.9 (rounded to one decimal)Mid 2030 all tech 2,475.1 less mid 2030 fabs 454.7 = 2,020.4 aMW (workbook values).

  2. Council releases initial 20-year forecast for Pacific Northwest electricity demand

    Northwest Power and Conservation Council, published 05/02/2025. Agency figure.

    Boundary: Pacific Northwest as the Council defines it: Oregon, Idaho, Washington, and Western Montana.

    ClaimMeasureFigureValue yearQuote
    Verified against the source.
    dem-058Pacific Northwest electricity demand, NW Council 2025 forecast: Current annual energy22,000 aMW2025The Pacific Northwest – which the Council defines as Oregon, Idaho, Washington, and Western Montana – has consumed approximately 22,000 aMW (average megawatts) of electricity per year for the last several years.
    dem-061Pacific Northwest electricity demand, NW Council 2025 forecast: Caveat on load forecast2046The Council urged the public to exercise caution in interpreting this analysis, because it shows higher levels of electricity consumption in peak demand and in annual energy than will actually occur.
    Partly verified. Limit: This is the Council's spring 2025 initial forecast; its August 2026 draft Ninth Plan gives a lower and narrower range, so this one is shown only as the earlier forecast.
    dem-059Pacific Northwest electricity demand, NW Council 2025 forecast: Annual energy by 204631,000 to 44,000 aMW2046The forecast projects annual energy demand growth to reach between 31,000-44,000 aMW by 2046, varying by trajectory.

  3. Draft Ninth Power Plan, Technical Appendix F: Electricity Demand Forecast

    Northwest Power and Conservation Council, published 08/17/2026. Value year 2046. Agency figure.

    ClaimMeasureFigureQuoteArithmetic
    Boundary: Pacific Northwest as the Council defines it: Oregon, Idaho, Washington, and Western Montana. One future of five; data center load is an estimate built from EIA sales data and utility data and likely differs from actual values. Verified against the source.
    dem-062Pacific Northwest data centers, NW Council draft Ninth Plan (Mixed Bag, Climate A): Total aMW growth 2025 to 2046, all sectors11,937 aMWIn this future, the total growth in average MW (aMW) from 2025 to 2046 is 11,937 aMW.
    Boundary: Pacific Northwest as the Council defines it: Oregon, Idaho, Washington, and Western Montana. One future of five. Verified against the source.
    dem-063Pacific Northwest data centers, NW Council draft Ninth Plan (Mixed Bag, Climate A): Share of 2025 to 2046 load growth from data centers20 percent20% is from data centers0.20 x 11,937 aMW = about 2,387 aMW of data center growth by 2046 in this future.
    Boundary: Pacific Northwest as the Council defines it: Oregon, Idaho, Washington, and Western Montana. All sectors, not data centers alone; climate pattern A futures. Partly verified. Limit: The source prints the high end as 17.820, which is read here as 17,820 because the low end uses a comma; the range is growth across all sectors, not data centers alone.
    dem-071Pacific Northwest electricity demand, NW Council draft Ninth Plan Appendix F: Total energy growth range across futures, 2025 to 20467,645 to 17,820 aMWIn terms of total energy growth in average MW, the futures range from a high of 17.820 aMW to a low of 7,645 aMW.On the 22,000 aMW base, 2046 would be about 29,600 to 39,800 aMW (our arithmetic; the Council's own 2025 base for this forecast was not read).
    Boundary: Pacific Northwest as the Council defines it: Oregon, Idaho, Washington, and Western Montana. All sectors, not data centers alone; climate pattern A futures. The 22,000 aMW base is the figure in dem-058; the Council's own base year for this forecast was not read. Partly verified. Limit: These totals add the Council's growth range to 22,000 average megawatts, a base the Council does not state for this forecast, and the source prints the high end as 17.820, which is read here as 17,820.
    dem-092Pacific Northwest electricity demand, 2046 regional total (growth range added to today's 22,000 aMW): Regional load in 2046 if the Council's 2025 to 2046 growth range is added to 22,000 aMW today29,600 to 39,800 aMWIn terms of total energy growth in average MW, the futures range from a high of 17.820 aMW to a low of 7,645 aMW.22,000 aMW (dem-058) + 7,645 = 29,645; 22,000 + 17,820 = 39,820; each rounded to the nearest 100 gives about 29,600 to 39,800 aMW.

  4. 2026 Northwest Regional Forecast of Power Loads and Resources

    Pacific Northwest Utilities Conference Committee, published 04/22/2026. Value year 2036. Company-reported figure.

    Boundary: Utility-reported loads after losses and efficiency; August 2026 through July 2036; not data centers alone.

    The annual energy projection shown in the green solid line suggests that electricity demand could grow by approximately 7,900 average megawatts (aMW) over the next 10 years, reflecting continued expansion in large loads as well as broader economic and demographic trends across the region.

    Verified against the source. Claim dem-068.

  5. 2026 Pacific Northwest Loads and Resources Study (White Book)

    Bonneville Power Administration, published 04/03/2026. Value year 2036. Agency figure.

    Boundary: PNW regional analysis of firm loads and resources (Table 3-8), firm water conditions, assuming 100 percent of the region's uncommitted IPP generation (about 2,600 aMW) is available to serve regional loads; mainly driven by rising retail loads, not tied to data centers in the text. OY 2027 shows a 337 aMW surplus.

    to have an annual energy surplus as large as 337 aMW in OY 2027, that sharply decrease to a deficit of 3,514 aMW by OY 2036. This result was mainly driven by the increasing PNW retail loads.

    Verified against the source. Claim dem-069.

  6. Summer Preparedness and Long-Term Resource Adequacy Meeting Summary 2026 (Docket UE-210096)

    Washington Utilities and Transportation Commission and Department of Commerce, published 09/01/2026. Value year 2030. Agency figure.

    Boundary: E3 study for Pacific Northwest utilities; load growth is mainly data centers plus electrification.

    Without any new resource additions, the region’s adequacy gap may begin in 2026 and grow to 9 gigawatts, approximately the load of the state of Oregon, by 2030.

    Verified against the source. Claim dem-070.

  7. 9th Power Plan

    Northwest Power and Conservation Council, published 11/04/2026. Value year 2026. Agency figure.

    Boundary: Pacific Northwest as the Council defines it: Oregon, Idaho, Washington, and Western Montana. Status of the plan document, not a forecast figure.

    The Council will be continuing to develop the Ninth Plan over the coming months, working towards a second draft for release in December or January. The draft Ninth Power Plan was released for public comment through October 16.

    Verified against the source. Claim dem-093.

  8. Power Committee Meeting, October 23, 2026 (Ninth Power Plan update)

    Northwest Power and Conservation Council, published 10/23/2026. Agency figure.

    ClaimMeasureFigureValue yearQuote
    Boundary: Pacific Northwest as the Council defines it: Oregon, Idaho, Washington, and Western Montana. A staff planning date, subject to change; the status page says 'December or January'. Partly verified. Limit: The slide text prints 'December 15-16 (Portland)' without a year; 2026 follows from the sequence after the 12/02 webinar on the same slide and from the 10/23/2026 date of the deck. A staff planning date that can move: slide 18 ('Public Comment and Finalizing a Plan') also lists January 12-13 and February 9-10 as alternative Draft 2 release meetings.
    dem-097Pacific Northwest, NW Council Ninth Power Plan (second draft timing): Potential release of a second draft2026December 15-16 (Portland) Potential release of a second draft Ninth Plan
    Boundary: Pacific Northwest as the Council defines it: Oregon, Idaho, Washington, and Western Montana. A staff planning timeline, not an adoption. Partly verified. Limit: The slide text prints the meeting dates without a year; 2027 is inferred from the sequence (it follows the 12/15-16/2026 second draft) and from the weekdays: 04/13/2027 and 05/11/2027 are Tuesdays, while in 2026 both dates are Mondays and the Council meets mid-week. A staff planning timeline that can move: the April 13-14 target is the staff working timeline (December Draft 2); April or May, and May 11-12, are fallbacks if the second draft slips to January or February.
    dem-098Pacific Northwest, NW Council Ninth Power Plan (final plan timing): Potential release of the final plan2027Staff is working towards this timeline for now | December Draft 2 Release | December 15-16 Meeting | 60-day | Mid-February | Use the March and April Meetings | April 13-14 meeting

Federal outlook, AEO2026

EIA's baseline puts data center servers at 105 billion kWh in 2025 and 178 by 2030

8 sources from 6 documents: 8 verified. Open the plate. Data: dc-eia-servers.json, with its claims in demand.json.

  1. EIA API v2, Annual Energy Outlook 2026 data route

    U.S. Energy Information Administration, published 04/08/2026. Value year 2025. Agency figure.

    Boundary: Electricity purchased for data center servers in the commercial sector (EIA end use 'Data Center Servers'), site electricity. Server cooling and ventilation are counted under other commercial end uses (space cooling, ventilation), so this is not whole-facility use. Not directly comparable with LBNL, which counts servers, storage, network equipment, and infrastructure. Cryptocurrency scope not stated by EIA.

    seriesName "Commercial : Purchased Electricity : Data Center Servers"; scenario "cb2026" (Counterfactual Baseline case); period 2025; value 0.357692 quads

    Verified against the source. Claim dem-001.

    Arithmetic: 0.3577 quads x 293.07 BkWh per quad (1e15 Btu / 3,412.14 Btu per kWh) = 104.8 BkWh. The series applies the same conversion to every year 2025 to 2050 and rounds each to one decimal.

  2. EIA API v2, Annual Energy Outlook 2026 data route

    U.S. Energy Information Administration, published 04/08/2026. Value year 2030. Agency figure.

    Boundary: Electricity purchased for data center servers in the commercial sector (EIA end use 'Data Center Servers'), site electricity. Server cooling and ventilation are counted under other commercial end uses (space cooling, ventilation), so this is not whole-facility use. Not directly comparable with LBNL, which counts servers, storage, network equipment, and infrastructure. Cryptocurrency scope not stated by EIA.

    seriesName "Commercial : Purchased Electricity : Data Center Servers"; scenario "higheldmd" (High Electricity Demand); period 2030; value 0.65083 quads

    Verified against the source. Claim dem-004.

    Arithmetic: 0.6508 quads x 293.07 BkWh per quad (1e15 Btu / 3,412.14 Btu per kWh) = 190.7 BkWh. The series applies the same conversion to every year 2025 to 2050 and rounds each to one decimal.

  3. EIA API v2, Annual Energy Outlook 2026 data route

    Boundary: Electricity purchased for data center servers in the commercial sector (EIA end use 'Data Center Servers'), site electricity. Server cooling and ventilation are counted under other commercial end uses (space cooling, ventilation), so this is not whole-facility use.     U.S. Energy Information Administration, published 04/08/2026. Value year 2030. Agency figure.

  5. Annual Energy Outlook 2026, Narrative

    U.S. Energy Information Administration, published 04/08/2026. Value year 2050. Agency figure.

    Boundary: Electricity purchased for data center servers in the commercial sector (EIA end use 'Data Center Servers'), site electricity. Server cooling and ventilation are counted under other commercial end uses (space cooling, ventilation), so this is not whole-facility use. Not directly comparable with LBNL, which counts servers, storage, network equipment, and infrastructure. Cryptocurrency scope not stated by EIA.

    In 2050, the High Electricity Demand case shows 84% more data center server electricity use than the Counterfactual Baseline case.

    Verified against the source. Claim dem-015.

    Arithmetic: API values 818.7 / 445.6 = 1.837, so 84% higher.

  6. Data center server energy use grows across the commercial building stock (Today in Energy)

    U.S. Energy Information Administration, published 05/19/2026. Agency figure.

    Verified against the source.

    ClaimMeasureFigureValue yearQuoteArithmetic
    Boundary: Servers only, as a share of commercial sector electricity consumption.
    dem-016US data center servers, EIA AEO2026: Server share of commercial sector electricity, 20257 percent2025Across all cases, servers alone accounted for an estimated 7% of commercial sector electricity consumption in 2025.API: 104.8 BkWh servers / 1,510.3 BkWh commercial purchased electricity (Table 5 'Electricity Subtotal') = 6.9%.
    Boundary: Servers only, share of commercial building electricity.
    dem-017US data center servers, EIA AEO2026: Server share of commercial building electricity, 205022 to 33 percent2050Data center server electricity use grows to 22%–33% of commercial building electricity use by 2050 across our cases.API: Counterfactual Baseline 445.6 / 1,983.0 = 22.5%; High Electricity Demand 818.7 / 2,461.3 = 33.3%. The denominator is commercial purchased electricity, not retail sales (2,298 BkWh in cb2026, which would give 19.4%).
    Boundary: Operational power draw per server, Counterfactual Baseline only.
    dem-021US data center servers, EIA AEO2026 Counterfactual Baseline: Assumed efficiency gain after 204010 percent per three years2040In our Counterfactual Baseline case, we assume that after 2040, servers will become increasingly efficient, resulting in a 10% reduction in average annual operational power draw every three years, above and beyond historical efficiency trends.

The whole building, 2025 Update

LBNL counts 192 TWh for US data centers in 2024 and 649 by 2030 in its reference case

11 sources from 4 documents: 11 verified. Open the plate. Data: dc-lbnl-us.json, with its claims in demand.json and tradeoffs.json.

  1. United States Data Center Energy Usage Report: 2025 Update

    Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory (hosted by U.S. Department of Energy), published 06/18/2026. Agency figure.

    Verified against the source.

    ClaimMeasureFigureValue yearQuoteArithmetic
    Boundary: Servers, storage, network equipment, and infrastructure (cooling and power) in US data centers; excludes cryptocurrency mining. Electricity use, not power capacity.
    dem-028US data centers, LBNL 2025 Update: Electricity use, 2024 (last historical year)192 TWh2024The updated estimate of data center electricity use in 2024 is 192 terawatt-hours (TWh), accounting for 4.7% of total U.S. electricity consumption.
    dem-033US data centers, LBNL 2025 Update Reference Case: Electricity use, 2028464 TWh2028Meanwhile, our current Reference Case forecast for total electricity use in 2028 is 464 TWh, which falls in the middle of the range presented in the 2024 Report.
    dem-029US data centers, LBNL 2025 Update Reference Case: Electricity use, 2030649 TWh2030The Reference Case estimate for 2030 data center electricity use is 649 TWh, representing 11.8% of forecasted 2030 U.S. electricity use as presented in the 2025 North American Electric Reliability Corporation (NERC) Long-Term Reliability Assessment (NERC, 2026).649 / 192 = 3.38x the 2024 level; implied growth (649/192)^(1/6) - 1 = 22.5% per year.
    dem-030US data centers, LBNL 2025 Update: Electricity use range, 2030 (Compounded Uncertainty low to high)521 to 843 TWh2030This yields a Compounded Uncertainty Range of 521–843 TWh, equal to 9.5–15.3% of total U.S. electricity use in 2030.
    Boundary: Capacity (not electricity) implied by the Reference Case at an assumed 50 percent average utilization of interconnection capacity.
    dem-034US data centers, LBNL 2025 Update Reference Case: Interconnection capacity needed, 2030148 GW2030Assuming a 50% average utilization rate, the Reference Case electricity use projections translate to 148 gigawatts (GW) of interconnection capacity needed to serve data centers in 2030.649 TWh / 8,760 h = 74.1 GW average load; 74.1 / 0.5 = 148 GW.
    Boundary: Uses NERC forecasts as the denominator and assumes they include data center use consistent with LBNL.
    dem-035US data centers, LBNL 2025 Update Reference Case: Share of total US load growth, 2024 to 203033 percent2030This would result in data centers being responsible for 33% of total load growth over the six-year period.Data center growth 649 - 192 = 457 TWh; non-data-center growth 926 TWh; 457 / (457 + 926) = 33%.
    Boundary: Servers, storage, network equipment and infrastructure in US data centers; excludes cryptocurrency mining. Revised history, not the 2024 Report's series.
    tr-064US data centers, LBNL 2025 Update: growth in electricity use, 2023 to 2024 (revised history)14 percent2024The continued deployment of GPU- and ASIC- accelerated servers, with ever-increasing computing capabilities and power consumption, led to a 14% increase in U.S. data center electricity use between 2023 and 2024.192 TWh in 2024 (dem-028) / 1.14 = about 168 TWh implied for 2023, against 176 TWh in the 2024 Report (dem-036), a revision of about -4.5% using the rounded 168, or -4.3% unrounded (168.4 / 176). The 14% is itself rounded, so 2023 lies between 167.7 and 169.2 TWh. LBNL does not print a 2023 value in the text read, so 168 is our arithmetic.

  2. United States Data Center Energy Usage Report: 2025 Update (abstract)

    Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory, published 06/01/2026. Value year 2030. Agency figure.

    Boundary: Servers, storage, network equipment, and infrastructure (cooling and power) in US data centers; excludes cryptocurrency mining. Electricity use, not power capacity.

    Verified against the source.

    ClaimMeasureFigureQuoteArithmetic
    dem-031US data centers, LBNL 2025 Update, Consolidated Deployment sensitivity: Electricity use, 2030578 TWhLowering the forecasted installations of data center IT equipment (578 TWh in 2030)578 / 649 - 1 = -10.9% by our arithmetic; LBNL's own table prints -10.8% (Consolidated Deployment).
    dem-032US data centers, LBNL 2025 Update, High Inference Energy sensitivity: Electricity use, 2030782 TWhIncreasing the idle power and utilization rates of AI servers (782 TWh in 2030)782 / 649 - 1 = +20.5% by our arithmetic; LBNL's own table prints +20.6% (High Inference Energy).

  3. Powering Intelligence: Updated U.S. Data Center Scenarios

    Electric Power Research Institute, published 03/02/2026. Value year 2030. Company-reported figure.

    Boundary: US data centers, EPRI scenarios (nominal IT capacity and electricity use); EPRI's own boundary and method.

    In 2024, total U.S. data center electricity use is estimated at 177-192 TWh, growing to roughly 380-790 TWh by 2030.

    Verified against the source. Claim dem-051.

  4. Six Things to Know About BNEF's New US Data Center Capacity Outlook

    BloombergNEF, published 07/29/2026. Value year 2030. Company-reported figure.

    Boundary: Installed US data center capacity in GW as tracked by BloombergNEF (project-based forecast); capacity, not electricity use.

    BloombergNEF’s forecasted 2030 installed US data center capacity is now 118 gigawatts (GW), an upwards revision of 52% compared to our December 2025 forecast.

    Verified against the source. Claim dem-053.

    Arithmetic: 118 / 1.52 = 77.6 GW implied December 2025 value for 2030.

The world

The IEA counts 485 TWh for the world's data centers in 2025 and about 945 by 2030 in its base case

8 sources from 3 documents: 7 verified and 1 partly verified. Open the plate. Data: dc-iea-world.json, with its claims in demand.json and tradeoffs.json.

  1. Energy and AI

    International Energy Agency, published 04/09/2025. Agency figure.

    Verified against the source.

    ClaimMeasureFigureValue yearQuoteArithmetic
    Boundary: Global data centres, about 1.5 percent of world electricity.
    dem-046Global data centres, IEA Energy and AI (2025): Electricity use, 2024415 TWh2024Data centres accounted for around 1.5% of the world’s electricity consumption in 2024, or 415 terawatt-hours (TWh).
    Boundary: Global data centres, Base Case.
    dem-048Global data centres, IEA Energy and AI (2025) Base Case (2026 report restates the same 945 in Annex Table A.4): Electricity use, 2030945 TWh2030Data centre electricity consumption is set to more than double to around 945 TWh by 2030.
    Boundary: Global data centres by region: US 45, China 25, Europe 15 percent.
    dem-047Global data centres, IEA Energy and AI (2025): United States share of global data centre electricity, 202445 percent2024The United States accounted for the largest share of global data centre electricity consumption in 2024 (45%), followed by China (25%) and Europe (15%).0.45 x 415 TWh = about 187 TWh, close to LBNL's 192 TWh for the US in 2024 (different boundaries and methods).

  2. Key Questions on Energy and AI

    International Energy Agency, published 04/16/2026. Agency figure.

    ClaimMeasureFigureValue yearQuoteArithmetic
    Boundary: Global data centres including AI-focused ones; IEA Base Case, Annex Table A.4 lists 485 TWh for 2025. The earlier sentence that IEA calls its 2026 values estimates was not found in the report and was removed. Verified against the source.
    dem-040Global data centres, IEA Key Questions on Energy and AI: Electricity use, 2025485 TWh2025Our updated projections see electricity consumption from data centres roughly doubling from 485 TWh in 2025 to 950 TWh in 2030, accounting for around 3% of global electricity demand by that date.
    Boundary: Global data centres, central projection; one of four cases. Partly verified. Limit: The IEA text says 950 TWh for 2030 while its own annex table prints 945 TWh; both are the same central case, so the page shows about 945 to 950.
    dem-041Global data centres, IEA Key Questions on Energy and AI central projection: Electricity use, 2030950 TWh2030Our updated projections see electricity consumption from data centres roughly doubling from 485 TWh in 2025 to 950 TWh in 2030, accounting for around 3% of global electricity demand by that date.950 / 485 = 1.96x; (950/485)^(1/5) - 1 = 14.4% per year.
    Boundary: Global data centres. Verified against the source.
    dem-042Global data centres, IEA: Electricity growth in 202517 percent2025The global electricity demand of data centres – the critical infrastructure for training and running AI models – grew by 17% in 2025, in line with IEA projections.
    Boundary: AI-focused data centres, global. Verified against the source.
    dem-043Global AI-focused data centres, IEA: Electricity growth in 202550 percent2025Electricity consumption from AI-focused data centres grew even faster, surging 50% in 2025.

  3. Powering Artificial Intelligence

    International Energy Agency, published 10/14/2026. Value year 2026. Agency figure.

    Boundary: Electricity-related CO2 of data centres, globally; operational only, no embodied carbon. value = around 150 Mt now; high = around 350 Mt by 2035 in the central scenario, raised because of higher onsite natural gas use. About 1.5% of global energy-related emissions in 2035.

    Data centres are currently associated with around 150 million tonnes of CO2 emissions due to their electricity consumption.

    Verified against the source. Claim tr-035.

    Arithmetic: 350 / 150 = 2.3x by 2035. Second quote: 'Our central scenario sees this growing to around 350 million tonnes by 2035'.

Past projections, checked

15 editions of 18 put 2023 US electricity sales high; 7 editions of 9 put commercial sales low

44 sources from 22 documents: 37 verified and 7 partly verified. Open the plate. Data: dc-forecast-check.json, with its claims in demand.json.

  1. Annual Energy Outlook 2013 Reference case, as compiled in the AEO Retrospective 2025 data file

    U.S. Energy Information Administration, published 04/15/2013. Agency figure.

    Verified against the source. Limit: EIA revises history after each edition's cutoff, so part of an older edition's gap is a later history revision and not a model error. Example read from the same files: AEO2017 shows 3,726.06 billion kWh for 2015 where the current series shows 3,758.99.

    ClaimMeasureFigureValue yearQuoteArithmetic

  15. 2024 United States Data Center Energy Usage Report (Figure 5.8)

    Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory, published 12/19/2024. Value year 2020. Agency figure.

    Boundary: US data centers: servers, storage, network equipment, and infrastructure (cooling and power), electricity use; the 2024 and 2025 reports exclude cryptocurrency mining. The 2016 report's stated scope is servers, storage, network equipment, and infrastructure in all US data centers; whether it counted cryptocurrency mining was not checked. Level comparison on LBNL's own figure; the 2014 base was restated from 70 to about 60 TWh, so the level gap mixes a method revision with real growth (see dem-107).

    Based on current trend estimates, U.S. data centers are projected to consume approximately 73 billion kWh in 2020. [2016 report] || [2024 Report, Figure 5.8: line read at 2020, about 105 TWh]
    ClaimMeasureFigureArithmetic
    Partly verified. Limit: Inherits the plus or minus 2 TWh read precision; a later historical estimate can itself be revised, and the 2025 Update already revised the 2024 Report's series downward.
    dem-108US data centers, LBNL 2016 projection for 2020 against 2024 Report estimate: Signed difference for 2020, realized estimate minus the 2016 Current Trends projection32 TWh1 billion kWh = 1 TWh, so the 2016 projection is 73 TWh (dem-039). Signed difference, realized minus projection: 105 - 73 = +32 TWh. Absolute percent error: 32 / 105 = 30.5 percent (realized estimate: dem-103; percent error: dem-109). The projection was below the realized estimate.
    Partly verified. Limit: Inherits the plus or minus 2 TWh read precision and the 2014 base revision.
    dem-109US data centers, LBNL 2016 projection for 2020 against 2024 Report estimate: Absolute percent error for 2020 (share of the realized estimate)30.5 percent|105 - 73| / 105 x 100 = 32 / 105 x 100 = 30.5 percent. Denominator is the realized estimate, as in the EIA claims. With plus or minus 2 TWh read precision the range is about 29 to 32 percent.

  16. 2024 United States Data Center Energy Usage Report

    Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory, published 12/19/2024. Agency figure.

    ClaimMeasureFigureValue yearQuoteArithmetic
    Boundary: US data centers: servers, storage, network equipment, and infrastructure (cooling and power), electricity use; the 2024 and 2025 reports exclude cryptocurrency mining. A comparison of the 2016 report and this current analysis is presented in Figure 5.8. [Figure 5.8 caption:] Total electricity use for 2014–2020 as estimated in this report, compared to the “Current Trends” scenario from the 2016 report.No sentence or table in the report gives a 2020 value. Read from the Figure 5.8 raster at its 2020 gridline against the y-axis ticks (0 TWh at image row 394, 120 TWh at row 39; the line at 2020 is centred on row 82.5): (394 - 82.5) / (394 - 39) x 120 = 105.3 TWh, recorded as 105. The same method gives 77.6 TWh for 2018 where the report's text says about 76, so allow plus or minus 2 TWh. The 2016 Current Trends line in the same figure reads about 73 at 2020, matching the 2016 report's 73 billion kWh (dem-039).
    Boundary: US data centers: servers, storage, network equipment, and infrastructure (cooling and power), electricity use; the 2024 and 2025 reports exclude cryptocurrency mining. The 2016 report's stated scope is servers, storage, network equipment, and infrastructure in all US data centers; whether it counted cryptocurrency mining was not checked. 'About 60 TWh' is the report's phrase for 2014 to 2016 together. Verified against the source. Limit: An approximate value ('about 60 TWh') covering 2014 to 2016. The 58 TWh figure in a DOE news summary was not found in the report text (see dem-038).
    dem-105US data centers, LBNL 2024 Report, restated 2014 base: Electricity use, 2014 (restated; the 2016 report said 70)60 TWh2014Data center energy use remained fairly stable between 2014–2016 at about 60 TWh. [Section 5, Comparison to Previous Report:] Even looking at the historical base year of that study, 2014, we have slightly different estimates for total electricity consumption. [...] About one-third of the difference between the 2014 values in the two studies is due to the differences in server electricity consumption described above.2016 report 2014 value 70 billion kWh (dem-038) = 70 TWh.     Boundary: US data centers, 2024 method; growth over the six years, from about 60 TWh in 2014 to about 105 TWh in 2020. Partly verified. Limit: Built from one approximate text value and one chart reading. A growth comparison removes the 2014 base revision, which the level comparison does not.

  18. United States Data Center Energy Usage Report: 2025 Update

    Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory, published 06/18/2026. Value year 2020. Agency figure.

    Boundary: US data centers: servers, storage, network equipment, and infrastructure (cooling and power), electricity use; the 2024 and 2025 reports exclude cryptocurrency mining. The 2016 report's stated scope is servers, storage, network equipment, and infrastructure in all US data centers; whether it counted cryptocurrency mining was not checked. The Update keeps the 2024 Report's method and revises the historical series slightly downward.

    The updated estimate of data center electricity use in 2024 is 192 terawatt-hours (TWh), accounting for 4.7% of total U.S. electricity consumption. This falls at the low end of the estimated range in the 2024 Report, with revised estimates of electricity use in prior years falling slightly below 2024 Report estimates.

    Partly verified. Limit: Read from a plotted line, not a published number; precision about plus or minus 2 TWh. Claim dem-104.

    Arithmetic: Read from the Figure 1 raster at its 2020 gridline: 0 TWh at image row 751.5, 800 TWh at row 86, line at 2020 centred on row 665.5: (751.5 - 665.5) / (751.5 - 86) x 800 = 103.4 TWh, recorded as 103. The same method gives 76.3 for 2018 (the report says about 76) and 191.7 for 2024 (the report says 192), so allow plus or minus 2 TWh.

Seven tradeoffs

Power, water, and carbon have agency figures; land, heat, and materials have one or two modeled ones

  1. 2024 United States Data Center Energy Usage Report

    Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory (LBNL-2001637), published 12/19/2024. Value year 2023. Agency figure.

    Verified against the source.

    ClaimMeasureFigureQuoteArithmetic
    Boundary: Onsite (site WUE) consumption by all US data centers, modeled bottom-up. Excludes water used to generate the electricity (see tr-004) and chip manufacturing. Includes hyperscale, colocation and internal (enterprise) data centers.
    tr-001US data centers (LBNL model): direct (onsite) water consumption, total66 billion litersBy 2023, hyperscale and colocation account for 84% of the 66-billion-liter total, while internal data centers fell to just 12%, driven by water efficiency improvements.84% x 66 = 55.4 billion L hyperscale and colocation; 12% x 66 = 7.9 billion L internal.
    Boundary: Water consumed at power plants for the electricity data centers used, using the regional grid mix of data center locations. Does not reflect power purchase agreements or behind-the-meter generation. Excludes chip manufacturing.
    tr-004US data centers (LBNL model): indirect water consumption through electricity use800 billion liters (nearly)The total indirect water footprint of U.S. data centers is nearly 800 billion liters, attributed to water consumed indirectly through electricity use, based on the regional electricity grid mix for U.S. data center locations.Indirect / direct = 800 / 66 = 12.1x. Intensity check: 800e9 L / 176e9 kWh = 4.5 L/kWh, matching the 4.52 L/kWh in tr-005.
    Boundary: Site (onsite) WUE only, averaged over all US data centers. value = 2014 to 2023 plateau ('just over 0.36'); low to high = range reached after 2023 in the scenario years.
    tr-009US data centers (LBNL model): average site WUE (water per kWh of IT energy)0.36 (0.45 to 0.48) L/kWhAfter 2023, the average WUE rises slightly, reaching between 0.45 and 0.48 L/kWh, reflecting increasing WUEs in the hyperscale and colocation data centers, along with the increased water consumption of liquid-cooled systems.
    Boundary: Grid-average water consumed per kWh at the generator, for data center locations. US all-sector average is 4.35 L/kWh in the same report.
    tr-005US data centers (LBNL model): indirect water intensity of data center electricity4.52 L/kWhIn 2023, energy consumption by a data center translates to national average of 4.52 L/kWh of indirect water consumption, along with 0.34 kg/ kWh emissions.
    Boundary: Scope 2 style: emissions of the grid mix serving data center locations, location-based. Excludes embodied carbon of buildings and hardware, and excludes PPAs and onsite generation.
    tr-006US data centers (LBNL model): greenhouse gas emissions from electricity use61 billion kg CO2e (61 Mt)Concurrently, total GHG emissions for that same electricity grid mix would be 61 billion kilograms of CO2 equivalent.61e9 kg / 176e9 kWh = 0.35 kg/kWh, consistent with the 0.34 kg/kWh quoted.

  2. United States Data Center Energy Usage Report: 2025 Update

    Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory (LBNL-2001758), published 06/18/2026. Value year 2024. Agency figure.

    The updated estimate of data center electricity use in 2024 is 192 terawatt-hours (TWh), accounting for 4.7% of total U.S. electricity consumption.

    Partly verified. Limit: LBNL does not publish this figure. It multiplies LBNL's electricity for the year (2025 Update) by LBNL's 2023 national intensity (2024 Report), so it applies a 2023 grid mix to a later year and any change in the mix is not captured. Modeled, location-based, excludes power purchase agreements and onsite generation.

    ClaimMeasureFigureArithmetic
    Boundary: Water consumed at power plants for the electricity US data centers used in 2024, at the 2023 national data center intensity of 4.52 L/kWh. Same boundary as tr-004. Excludes chip manufacturing.
    tr-068US data centers (derived: LBNL 2025 Update electricity x LBNL 2024 Report water intensity): indirect water consumption through electricity use, rebased to the 2025 Update868 billion liters192 TWh = 192e9 kWh; 192e9 kWh x 4.52 L/kWh = 867.8e9 L, about 868 billion L. At the US all-sector average of 4.35 L/kWh (tr-005 boundary): 192e9 x 4.35 = 835.2e9 L. Against the 2024 Report's 2023 figure: 868 / 795.5 (176e9 x 4.52) = +9.1%; against the printed 'nearly 800': +8.5%.
    Boundary: Scope 2 style, location-based: emissions of the grid mix serving data center locations, at the 2023 intensity of 0.34 kg CO2e/kWh. Same boundary as tr-006. Excludes embodied carbon of buildings and hardware, power purchase agreements and onsite generation.
    tr-070US data centers (derived: LBNL 2025 Update electricity x LBNL 2024 Report carbon intensity): greenhouse gas emissions from electricity use, rebased to the 2025 Update65.3 billion kg CO2e (65 Mt)192e9 kWh x 0.34 kg/kWh = 65.28e9 kg, about 65.3 billion kg (65 Mt). Using the unrounded 2024 Report ratio 61e9 / 176e9 = 0.3466 kg/kWh: 192e9 x 0.34659 = 66.545e9 kg, about 66.5 billion kg. Against the 2024 Report's 61 billion kg for 2023: 65.3 / 61 = +7.0%.

  10. The data heat island effect: quantifying the impact of AI data centers in a warming world (arXiv 2603.20897 v3)

    Marinoni et al. (preprint, not peer reviewed), published 03/21/2026. Value year 2026. Preprint, not peer reviewed.

    Boundary: Satellite land surface temperature, not air temperature, around AI data centers worldwide, before versus after operation. The abstract also states that more than 340 million people could be affected. Surface material (buildings, pavement) is a possible confounder; a critique argues the result could be land cover, not exhaust heat.

    We estimate that the land surface temperature increases by 2°C on average after the start of operations of an AI data centre, inducing local microclimate zones

    Partly verified. Limit: A preprint that has not been peer reviewed; it measures satellite land surface temperature, not air temperature, and buildings and pavement may account for part of the change. Claim tr-053.

  11. Azure Heat Reuse infographic

    Microsoft datacenters, published 03/01/2026. Value year 2026. Company-reported figure.

Households-heated estimates (for example 40 MW IT capacity gives 290 to 362 GWh a year, 83,000 to 104,000 Danish households) are Microsoft's equivalency using 3,475 kWh per Danish household; they assume a district heating network is in place.

    For every 1 MWh of electrical energy consumed, between 0.69 MWh (in the winter) and 0.86 MWh (in the summer) of heat energy can be reused in an air-cooled datacenter.

    Partly verified. Limit: A potential from Microsoft's own modeling, not deployed heat reuse; it needs a nearby heat customer, and the publication date of 03/2026 is taken from the file path. Claim tr-061.

    Arithmetic: 290 GWh / 3,475 kWh per household = 83,500 households; 362 GWh / 3,475 = 104,200.

  14. How Big is the AI Waste Wave? (The Coming AI Waste Wave, Part 1)

    Basel Action Network, published 09/16/2026. Value year 2030. Modeled estimate.

    Boundary: Counts the full electro-mechanical infrastructure (servers and accelerators about 13% by weight, plus power, cooling, networking, storage), about 70,000 tonnes per GW of capacity, and a 'Contagion effect' of early scrap. Includes more than data center hardware. An NGO scenario estimate, not measurement; the comparator is de Vries and Gao 2026, which counted only servers and GPUs.

    By 2030, this study's estimate of AI-driven electronic equipment being retired (8.6–13.1 Mt/yr) is roughly 40–60 times higher than the most widely cited academic projection (de Vries and Gao, 2026), primarily because prior work counted only servers and GPUs and did not consider the Contagion effect.

    Partly verified. Limit: A scenario estimate from an advocacy organization's white paper, not a measurement; it counts all AI infrastructure electronics, not only servers. Claim tr-057.

    Arithmetic: Implied comparator: 8.6 / 40 = 0.22 and 13.1 / 60 = 0.22 million t per year.

Jobs

A 100 MW site implies about 70 to 200 construction workers at peak and 15 to 35 permanent staff

31 sources from 13 documents: 17 verified and 14 partly verified. Open the plate. Data: dc-jobs-per-mw.json, with its claims in jobs.json.

  1. Megawatts, but few permanent jobs? An agent-assisted evidence review of the data-centre construction cliff

    Smart and Sustainable Built Environment (Emerald), published 09/02/2026. Value year 2026. Peer-reviewed study.

    Evidence shows a sharp phase contrast: roughly 0.7–2.0 peak construction workers/MW (projected 1.3–3.6) versus 0.15–0.35 operational FTE/MW.
    ClaimMeasureFigure
    Boundary: Bottom-up modeled range for peak on-site construction headcount per megawatt, from an agent-assisted evidence synthesis (44 rows from 20 sources, no independent double coding); direct on-site workers; excludes multiplier jobs. The paper says estimates were compared within, not pooled across, evidence families, so this is a range from one family, not a pooled mean Partly verified. Limit: A modeled range for direct on-site workers from a synthesis of studies that the paper compares within, and does not pool across, evidence families; developer-announced figures run higher, at 1.3 to 3.6 per megawatt.
    jb-023U.S. data center construction: peak construction workers per MW0.7 to 2 workers per MW
    Boundary: Permanent on-site operating staff per megawatt; direct jobs only; excludes supply-chain and induced jobs; the paper compares estimates within evidence families and does not pool them Partly verified. Limit: Direct permanent staff only, from the same synthesis; the range matches another benchmark, so the two are not independent.
    jb-024U.S. data center operations: operational full-time equivalents per MW0.15 to 0.35 FTE per MW

    The Conversation, published 10/27/2026. Value year 2023. Press report.

  1. Microsoft 2026 Environmental Sustainability Report (FY2025)

    Microsoft, published 07/03/2026. Value year 2025. Company-reported figure.

    Boundary: Fiscal year 2025 (to 06/30/2025), Microsoft-owned datacenters. WUE is datacenter water use for cooling and humidification divided by energy use. 25% reduction versus the 2022 baseline; 2030 target is 40%.

    Verified against the source. Claim tr-028.

    Arithmetic: Implied 2022 baseline: 0.27 / 0.75 = 0.36 L/kWh. Microsoft's blog gives the long run: 'Our average WUE has decreased from 2.3 L/kWh to 0.27 L/kWh in 2025' (blogs.microsoft.com, 06/24/2026).

    Google (Elsworth et al.), published 08/21/2025. Value year 2025. Company-reported figure.
